{
  "abstract_verbatim": "We give a fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme (FPRAS) for counting perfect matchings in arbitrary finite simple undirected graphs, resolving the general-graph perfect-matching approximation problem. The algorithm returns zero with certainty when no perfect matching exists. <a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme (FPRAS) for counting perfect matchings in arbitrary finite simple undirected graphs, resolving the general-graph perfect-matching approximation problem. The algorithm returns zero with certainty when no perfect matching exists. Otherwise, it achieves relative error <i>ε</i> with failure probability at most <i>δ</i> in worst-case bit time polynomial in the input length, $`\varepsilon ^{-1}`$, and $`\log\delta^{-1}`$.

- **claim-main-fpras** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a uniform classical randomized algorithm which, given $G$ and rational parameters $0<\varepsilon<1$ and $0<\delta<1/2$, returns a nonnegative rational number $\widehat Z$ such that $$\mathbb P\bigl[(1-\varepsilon)Z(G)\le \widehat Z\le(1+\varepsilon)Z(G)\bigr] \ge 1-\delta.$$ If $Z(G)=0$, the output is zero with certainty. The worst-case bit running time is polynomial in the input encoding length, $\varepsilon^{-1}$, and $\log\delta^{-1}$.
  - Explanation: A single uniform classical randomized procedure approximates the number of perfect matchings of any finite simple graph within the requested relative error and confidence. It outputs zero with certainty when no perfect matching exists, and its worst-case bit time is polynomial in the input length, the inverse error and the logarithm of the inverse failure probability.
- **claim-logical-hole-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-logical-hole-bound)): Let $\lambda$ be balanced, let $B$ be its bottleneck similarity, and set $$D_0=10^8(n+1)^4.$$ For every even set $U\subseteq V$, $$\label{eq:logical-hole-bound} g_\lambda(U)\Phi_B(U)\le 2D_0^{\|U\|/2}.$$
  - Explanation: For balanced activities, the hole ratio of any even vertex set multiplied by its pairing capacity is bounded by a constant raised to half the size of the set. Only the two-hole row maxima are controlled by balance; the bound extends this control to every even hole set.
- **claim-four-hole-inequality** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-four-holes)): For any positive activities $\mu$ and four distinct vertices $a,b,c,d$, $$g_\mu(abcd)\le g_\mu(ab)g_\mu(cd)+g_\mu(ac)g_\mu(bd) +g_\mu(ad)g_\mu(bc).$$ Consequently, if every row maximum is at most $4$, then $$\label{eq:small-hole-bounds} g_\mu(\varnothing)=1,\qquad g_\mu(U)\le4\quad(\|U\|=2),\qquad g_\mu(U)\le48\quad(\|U\|=4).$$
  - Explanation: The four-hole ratio is bounded by the sum over the three pairings of products of two-hole ratios, proved by switching alternating paths in an overlay of two matchings. Under bounded row maxima this gives explicit constant bounds for two and four holes.
- **claim-hole-relocation** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-hole-relocation)): Let $B$ be defined by <a href="#eq:bottleneck" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bottleneck">[eq:bottleneck]</a>, and suppose that $\mu_e\ge\lambda_e$ for every edge and $$\frac18\le r_i(\mu)\le4\qquad(i\in V).$$ If $\|U\|\in\{2,4\}$ and $i,j\in U$ are distinct, then $$\label{eq:hole-relocation-bound} g_\mu(U)\le\frac{10^5n}{B_{ij}}.$$
  - Explanation: When two holes are joined by a strong bottleneck path, the hole ratio of a small hole set is inversely proportional to the bottleneck similarity of that pair, with a polynomial prefactor in the number of vertices. The relocation errors add rather than multiply along the path.
- **claim-subdivision-bijection** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-subdivision)): The real graph has $N$ vertices. Its perfect matchings are in bijection with logical perfect matchings, and $$\label{eq:real-partition} Z'=C_0 Z_\lambda, \qquad C_0=\prod_e\prod_{\substack{1\le j\le4p+1\\j\text{ even}}}t_j^e.$$ On a noncentral real edge, its effective strength $B'$ equals its activity. On the central edge of the path for $uv$, it equals $B_{uv}$. Incident effective strengths differ by a factor of at most two. For an internal vertex $x_j$, its maximum incident effective strength is $$T_j= \begin{cases} t_j,&1\le j\le2p,\\ t_{j+1},&2p+1\le j\le4p. \end{cases}$$ Its *home terminal* is $u$ in the first case and $v$ in the second. Every edge on its leg to its home has activity at least $T_j$.
  - Explanation: Replacing each logical edge by a profiled path gives a graph whose perfect matchings are in bijection with the logical perfect matchings, with a common weight multiplier. Effective strengths at incident edges are comparable and each internal vertex has a home terminal.
- **claim-marked-laws** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-marked-laws)): For arbitrary positive logical activities, sample from the bag matching law and reveal the edge portions as above. The all-real event has probability $1/I$; conditional on this event, the decoded logical matching has law $\pi_\lambda$. For each logical edge $uv$, $$\label{eq:probe} \mathbb P(\text{probe for }uv\text{ is used and all other edges are real}) =\frac{g_\lambda(uv)}{DI}.$$ Conditional on this event, the residual logical matching has the $\lambda$-weighted perfect-matching law on the vertices other than $u,v$.
  - Explanation: Sampling from the bag matching law and revealing edge portions gives the real-only event with probability the reciprocal of the inflation factor, and conditional on it the logical matching follows the target law. The probe event for an edge exposes the corresponding two-hole ratio and leaves a weighted matching on the remaining vertices.
- **claim-bag-inflation** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-bag-inflation)): If the logical activities are balanced, then $$\label{eq:inflation} 1\le I\le2.$$
  - Explanation: Under balance, the auxiliary virtual edges increase the partition function by at most a factor of two, so the all-real event has probability at least one half.
- **claim-deletion-accounting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-deletion-accounting)): If an even hole set $U$ has a real completion, then $R$ is even, $\|R\|\le\|U\|$, and $$\label{eq:deletion-identity} Z'(-U) =C_0 F(U) Z_\lambda\bigl(-R;E_{\mathrm{br}}(U)\text{ forbidden}\bigr).$$ In particular, $g'(U)\le F(U)g_\lambda(R)$.
  - Explanation: Deleting internal path vertices is accounted for exactly by a logical hole set of consumed terminals and a product of neutral-pair and consumption factors. The hole ratio of the enlarged graph is therefore bounded by that factor times the logical hole ratio.
- **claim-ultrametric-pairing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-ultrametric-pairing)): Let $B\ge1$ be a symmetric similarity on a finite vertex set $X$, satisfying $B_{ij}\ge\min\{B_{ik},B_{kj}\}$. Let $\mathcal C_t$ be the equivalence classes of $B\ge t$, including singletons. For every even set $U\subseteq X$, $$\label{eq:threshold-pairing} \log\Phi_B(U) =\int_1^\infty\sum_{C\in\mathcal C_t} \left\lfloor\frac{\|C\cap U\|}{2}\right\rfloor\frac{dt}{t}.$$ There is one pairing that attains the maximum number of internal pairs in every threshold class simultaneously.
  - Explanation: For a similarity with the ultrametric-type property, the logarithm of the pairing capacity equals an integral over thresholds of the maximal internal pair counts of the threshold classes, and a single pairing attains all of them simultaneously.
- **claim-lifted-hole-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-lifted-hole-bound)): If the logical activities are balanced, then for every even set $U$ of enlarged vertices, $$g'(U)\Phi_{B'}(U)\le2D_0^{\|U\|/2}.$$ More precisely, for every $U$ with a real completion, the deterministic quantities above satisfy $$\label{eq:threshold-charge} F(U)\Phi_{B'}(U)\le\Phi_B(R).$$
  - Explanation: The logical hole bound transfers to the enlarged graph: the deletion factor times the enlarged pairing capacity is dominated by the pairing capacity of the logical hole set, which yields the same type of bound for every even enlarged hole set.
- **claim-quadrangulation** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-quadrangulation)): The polygon of $\mathcal C$ has a quadrangulation such that every cell side represents an admissible odd arc and the following hold in every cell:* 1. *some opposite pair of sides consists of good arcs;* 2. *if both arcs in an opposite pair are long, both arcs in the other opposite pair are good.* *There are $(m-2)/2$ cells and $(m-4)/2$ interior diagonals. One can construct the quadrangulation in time polynomial in $m$ and the encoding length of the label tree.* *Each diagonal may be assigned any common label from the fixed sets at its endpoints, used consistently in its two occurrences. For a short side, use the color of its actual cycle edge. These choices need not coincide with the auxiliary labels used in the construction.
  - Explanation: The polygon of a simple even cycle in the colored bag graph can be cut into quadrilateral cells whose sides are admissible odd arcs, each cell having a good opposite pair and the stated extra goodness when two opposite arcs are long. The construction is polynomial.
- **claim-cell-comparability** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cell-comparability)): Consider the four colored side chords of a cell, the first and last cycle edges of each of its four arcs, and one leaf repair for each good long arc. The labels of any two edges in this collection have tree distance at most $6$. Consequently their activities lie within a factor $$A_0=1000D$$ of one another. The same conclusion holds after clamping every edge activity to any common interval $[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)$.
  - Explanation: All chords, boundary edges and leaf repairs within one cell have labels at bounded tree distance, hence activities within a fixed factor, and this persists under any common clamp.
- **claim-pair-energy** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-pair-energy)): For every two real functions $f_1,f_2$ on $\mathcal M$, $$\label{eq:pair-energy} \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\pi f_1\le L\, \mathcal E\bigl((A,B)\mapsto f_1(A)+f_2(B)\bigr).$$ The same constant works for every common-interval clamp of the reference activities.
  - Explanation: The variance of any function of one matching coordinate is controlled by the Dirichlet energy of the additive two-coordinate function under the pair kernel, with a constant that also works for every common clamp of the activities.
- **claim-elementary-pair-energy** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-elementary-pair-energy)): With the notation above, $$\begin{aligned} \sum_A\pi(A)\sum_{A'\in\mathcal S(A)} (f_1(A)-f_1(A'))^2 &\le60N^2A_0^2 E, \label{eq:safe-switch-energy}\\ \sum_A\pi(A)\sum_{A'\in\mathcal K(A)} (f_1(A)-f_1(A'))^2 &\le12NA_0 E, \label{eq:color-energy}\\ \sum_{A,B}\pi(A)\pi(B)\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(A,B)} \bigl(F(A,B)-F((A,B)^C)\bigr)^2 &\le6NE. \label{eq:cycle-energy} \end{aligned}$$ Here $\mathcal C(A,B)$ denotes the discrepancy cycles, and $(A,B)^C$ is the pair after swapping $C$.
  - Explanation: Sums of squared differences over safe switches, color changes and cycle swaps are each bounded by the energy of the additive pair function times a polynomial factor, using proposal and acceptance lower bounds.
- **claim-cell-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-cell-identity)): The quantities just defined satisfy $$\label{eq:single-cell-identity} D_C=\Delta+\sum_{J\in P}g_J-\sum_{J\in Q}g_J+E_P-E_Q$$ for each cell. Consequently, $$\label{eq:global-cell-identity} D_C=\sum_{\text{cells of }C}(\Delta+E_P-E_Q).$$
  - Explanation: The whole-cycle difference decomposes exactly into local switch terms and context errors over the cells, because the signed gains of complementary arcs on each diagonal cancel against the cell count.
- **claim-encoding** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-encoding)): Every switch demand has an encoding $(A_P,B)$ by two perfect matchings, and every error demand has encodings $(A^{(j)},B^{(j)})$ for $j=0,1$, with the following properties.* 1. *For each encoding $(A,B)$, $\rho(d)\le A_0^4\pi(A)\pi(B)$.* 2. *For each encoding type and each fixed output pair, there are at most $\mathcal T$ demand preimages. This bound includes the choices of cycle, cell, opposite pair, and context.* 3. *For an error demand, both guide matchings satisfy $C_X\subseteq B^{(j)}$.
  - Explanation: Switch demands and error demands have encodings by pairs of perfect matchings with bounded weight distortion, a polynomial bound on the number of preimages, and a guide that retains the closed arc for error demands.
- **claim-swap-sum** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-swap-sum)): The sum in <a href="#eq:swap-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:swap-sum">[eq:swap-sum]</a> satisfies $\Sigma_{\rm swap}\le12N^2E$.
  - Explanation: The averaged context-error sum is bounded by the cycle-swap energy times a polynomial factor, since each identity corresponds to an entire discrepancy cycle of a pair.
- **claim-base-refresh** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-base-refresh)): The unit-activity partition function is computable by a dynamic program on the label tree. With exact rational categorical choices, its tables also produce an exact sample. The tables can be computed with $O((b_{\rm tree}+1)(N+1)^3)$ arithmetic operations on counts of $O(N\log(N+1))$ bits. Label and loop indices use $O(\log(b_{\rm tree}+1)+\log(N+1))$ additional bits. Once the tables are available, a sample uses polynomially many integer operations and at most $4(b_{\rm tree}+N+1)$ rational categorical choices, each with at most $N+1$ outcomes. A positive table also yields a matching by entirely deterministic backtracking.
  - Explanation: At unit activities the partition function and an exact uniform sample are computed by a dynamic program on the label tree with polynomial arithmetic and bounded bit sizes.
- **claim-replica-gap** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-replica-gap)): The lazy product chain has spectral gap at least $3/(8M)$, and hence at least $1/(20LM)$. The dependence on $L$ in the stronger bound is carried by $q=32L$, and hence by $M$.
  - Explanation: The lazy replicated product chain has a spectral gap inversely proportional to the number of updates, obtained by an ordered orthogonal decomposition and absorbing the residual interactions through replication.
- **claim-bag-sampler** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-thm-bag-sampler)): Define $T,q,M$ by [eq:ladder-parameters,eq:ladder-update-count] [eq:ladder-parameters](#eq:ladder-parameters), [eq:ladder-update-count](#eq:ladder-update-count), and put $$\begin{aligned} \Lambda&=10Nq(T+1) \bigl(K+\lceil\log_2(ND+2)\rceil+1\bigr), \label{eq:ladder-lambda}\\ s(\xi)&=\left\lceil20LM \bigl(\Lambda+\lceil\log_2(1/\xi)\rceil+1\bigr)\right\rceil \qquad(0<\xi<1). \label{eq:ladder-steps} \end{aligned}$$ Initialize all slots to any one colored perfect matching and run the exact chain for $s(\xi)$ steps. The resulting product law is at total variation distance at most $\xi$ from $\nu$. In particular, any top-tier slot has law within $\xi$ of the desired weighted matching law.* *All tables, state representations, and transition probabilities are computable by rational arithmetic. Their sizes, arithmetic operation counts, and rational bit lengths are polynomial in $N,D,K$, $b_{\rm tree}$, $\log(1/\xi)$, and the bit lengths of the reference activities. Exact rational choices here define an ideal process; the bounded-bit implementation in <a href="#sec:complexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:complexity">9</a> adds at most $\xi$ to the total variation error.
  - Explanation: Running the exact product chain for the stated number of steps from any initial state gives total variation distance at most the target from the stationary law, so a top-tier slot follows the desired weighted matching law; all computations are rational and polynomial.
- **claim-one-pass-scaling** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-one-pass-scaling)): Suppose $G$ is symmetric and $\alpha\le G_{ij}\le3$ for $i\ne j$. Initialize $v_i=2$ for every vertex and visit the vertices in their fixed order, setting $$\label{eq:scale-update} v_i\leftarrow\max_{j\ne i}\frac{G_{ij}}{v_j}.$$ Every coordinate only decreases, all final coordinates lie in $[\alpha/2,2]$, and the final vector satisfies $v_i v_j\ge G_{ij}$ with an equality in every row.
  - Explanation: A single visit of the vertices in a fixed order produces nonincreasing scale coordinates in a bounded interval that satisfy all product constraints with equality in every row.
- **claim-successful-stage** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-successful-stage)): Suppose the current logical activities are balanced and every empirical mean is within $\sigma$ of its exact mean. Then the next scaled activities are balanced and $$\label{eq:stage-relative-error} \left\|\frac{\widehat R}{R}-1\right\| \le10\sigma\le\frac\varepsilon{100K}.$$
  - Explanation: If the current activities are balanced and all empirical means are accurate, the next scaled activities are balanced and the partition ratio estimate has small relative error.
- **claim-finite-random-choice** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-random-choice)): Let $p_1,\ldots,p_r$ be nonnegative rational numbers summing to one. Choose a uniform integer $J\in\{0,\ldots,2^k-1\}$ using exactly $k$ fair bits, and return the least index $i$ such that $J/2^k<\sum_{j\le i}p_j$. The output law is at total variation distance at most $2r\,2^{-k}$ from $(p_i)_{i=1}^r$.
  - Explanation: A categorical choice implemented with a fixed number of fair bits has total variation error proportional to the number of outcomes times the grid resolution.
- **claim-prescribed-degree** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-cor-prescribed-degree)): For either family $\mathcal A=\mathcal F(G,f)$ or $\mathcal A=\mathcal M_k(G)$, there are uniform classical randomized algorithms with the following guarantees.* 1. *Given rational $0<\varepsilon<1$ and $0<\delta<1/2$, the counting algorithm returns a nonnegative rational $\widehat N$ such that $$\mathbb P[(1-\varepsilon)\|\mathcal A\|\le \widehat N\le(1+\varepsilon)\|\mathcal A\|] \ge1-\delta.$$ If $\mathcal A$ is empty, it returns zero with certainty. Its bit running time on every execution is polynomial in the full input encoding length, $\varepsilon^{-1}$, and $\log\delta^{-1}$.* 2. *Given rational $0<\tau<1$, the sampling algorithm reports infeasibility exactly when $\mathcal A$ is empty. Otherwise it returns a member $X$ of $\mathcal A$ on every execution, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal L(X),U_{\mathcal A})\le\tau,$$ where $U_{\mathcal A}$ is the uniform law on $\mathcal A$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu)=\frac12\sum_x\|\mu(x)-\nu(x)\|$. Its bit running time on every execution is polynomial in the full input encoding length and $\tau^{-1}$.
  - Explanation: Both prescribed-degree edge subsets and fixed-size matchings admit a randomized counting scheme with certain zero detection and a sampler within a chosen total variation distance, by constant-fibre reductions to perfect matchings.
- **claim-deletion-sampler** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-deletion-sampler)): Given a finite simple undirected unweighted graph $H_0$ whose vertices and edges are explicitly listed, and a rational $0<\tau<1$, there is a uniform classical randomized algorithm that reports infeasibility exactly when $H_0$ has no perfect matching. Otherwise it returns a perfect matching on every execution, and its output law is at total variation distance at most $\tau$ from the uniform perfect-matching law. Its bit running time on every execution is polynomial in the full input encoding length and $\tau^{-1}$.
  - Explanation: Edge-by-edge deletion with feasibility tests and approximate counts yields a perfect matching on every execution whose law is close in total variation to uniform, in time polynomial in the input and the inverse tolerance.

Its perfect matchings are in bijection with logical perfect matchings, and $$\\label{eq:real-partition}\n Z'=C_0 Z_\\lambda,\n \\qquad\n C_0=\\prod_e\\prod_{\\substack{1\\le j\\le4p+1\\\\j\\text{ even}}}t_j^e.$$ On a noncentral real edge, its effective strength $B'$ equals its activity. On the central edge of the path for $uv$, it equals $B_{uv}$. Incident effective strengths differ by a factor of at most two. For an internal vertex $x_j$, its maximum incident effective strength is $$T_j=\n \\begin{cases}\n t_j,&1\\le j\\le2p,\\\\\n t_{j+1},&2p+1\\le j\\le4p.\n \\end{cases}$$ Its *home terminal* is $u$ in the first case and $v$ in the second. Every edge on its leg to its home has activity at least $T_j$.",
          "source_id": "PM-113.1"
        }

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-marked-laws",
        "exact_quote": "For arbitrary positive logical activities, sample from the bag matching law and reveal the edge portions as above.       },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-ultrametric-pairing",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B\\ge1$ be a symmetric similarity on a finite vertex set $X$, satisfying $B_{ij}\\ge\\min\\{B_{ik},B_{kj}\\}$. Let $\\mathcal C_t$ be the equivalence classes of $B\\ge t$, including singletons.         "source_id": "PM-113.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-ultrametric-pairing",

          "source_id": "PM-113.1"
        }

        "exact_quote": "The polygon of $\\mathcal C$ has a quadrangulation such that every cell side represents an admissible odd arc and the following hold in every cell:*\n\n1.  *some opposite pair of sides consists of good arcs;*\n\n2.  *if both arcs in an opposite pair are long, both arcs in the other opposite pair are good.*\n\n*There are $(m-2)/2$ cells and $(m-4)/2$ interior diagonals.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-elementary-pair-energy",
        "exact_quote": "With the notation above, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\sum_A\\pi(A)\\sum_{A'\\in\\mathcal S(A)}\n       (f_1(A)-f_1(A'))^2\n &\\le60N^2A_0^2 E,\n \\label{eq:safe-switch-energy}\\\\\n \\sum_A\\pi(A)\\sum_{A'\\in\\mathcal K(A)}\n       (f_1(A)-f_1(A'))^2\n &\\le12NA_0 E,\n \\label{eq:color-energy}\\\\\n \\sum_{A,B}\\pi(A)\\pi(B)\\sum_{C\\in\\mathcal C(A,B)}\n       \\bigl(F(A,B)-F((A,B)^C)\\bigr)^2\n &\\le6NE.\n \\label{eq:cycle-energy}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here $\\mathcal C(A,B)$ denotes the discrepancy cycles, and $(A,B)^C$ is the pair after swapping $C$.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-encoding",
        "exact_quote": "Every switch demand has an encoding $(A_P,B)$ by two perfect matchings, and every error demand has encodings $(A^{(j)},B^{(j)})$ for $j=0,1$, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *For each encoding $(A,B)$, $\\rho(d)\\le A_0^4\\pi(A)\\pi(B)$.*\n\n2.  *For each encoding type and each fixed output pair, there are at most $\\mathcal T$ demand preimages.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-base-refresh",
        "exact_quote": "The unit-activity partition function is computable by a dynamic program on the label tree. With exact rational categorical choices, its tables also produce an exact sample. The tables can be computed with $O((b_{\\rm tree}+1)(N+1)^3)$ arithmetic operations on counts of $O(N\\log(N+1))$ bits. Label and loop indices use $O(\\log(b_{\\rm tree}+1)+\\log(N+1))$ additional bits. Once the tables are available, a sample uses polynomially many integer operations and at most $4(b_{\\rm tree}+N+1)$ rational categorical choices, each with at most $N+1$ outcomes. A positive table also yields a matching by entirely deterministic backtracking.",
        "source_id": "PM-113.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

Once the tables are available, a sample uses polynomially many integer operations and at most $4(b_{\\rm tree}+N+1)$ rational categorical choices, each with at most $N+1$ outcomes. A positive table also yields a matching by entirely deterministic backtracking.",
          "source_id": "PM-113.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-prescribed-degree",
        "exact_quote": "For either family $\\mathcal A=\\mathcal F(G,f)$ or $\\mathcal A=\\mathcal M_k(G)$, there are uniform classical randomized algorithms with the following guarantees.*\n\n1.  *Given rational $0<\\varepsilon<1$ and $0<\\delta<1/2$, the counting algorithm returns a nonnegative rational $\\widehat N$ such that $$\\mathbb P[(1-\\varepsilon)|\\mathcal A|\\le \\widehat N\\le(1+\\varepsilon)|\\mathcal A|]\n     \\ge1-\\delta.$$ If $\\mathcal A$ is empty, it returns zero with certainty. Its bit running time on every execution is polynomial in the full input encoding length, $\\varepsilon^{-1}$, and $\\log\\delta^{-1}$.*\n\n2.  *Given rational $0<\\tau<1$, the sampling algorithm reports infeasibility exactly when $\\mathcal A$ is empty. Otherwise it returns a member $X$ of $\\mathcal A$ on every execution, and $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{TV}}(\\mathcal L(X),U_{\\mathcal A})\\le\\tau,$$ where $U_{\\mathcal A}$ is the uniform law on $\\mathcal A$ and $\\mathop{\\mathrm{TV}}(\\mu,\\nu)=\\frac12\\sum_x|\\mu(x)-\\nu(x)|$. Its bit running time on every execution is polynomial in the full input encoding length and $\\tau^{-1}$.",
        "source_id": "PM-113.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-cor-prescribed-degree",
          "exact_quote": "For either family $\\mathcal A=\\mathcal F(G,f)$ or $\\mathcal A=\\mathcal M_k(G)$, there are uniform classical randomized algorithms with the following guarantees.*\n\n1.  *Given rational $0<\\varepsilon<1$ and $0<\\delta<1/2$, the counting algorithm returns a nonnegative rational $\\widehat N$ such that $$\\mathbb P[(1-\\varepsilon)|\\mathcal A|\\le \\widehat N\\le(1+\\varepsilon)|\\mathcal A|]\n     \\ge1-\\delta.$$ If $\\mathcal A$ is empty, it returns zero with certainty. Its bit running time on every execution is polynomial in the full input encoding length, $\\varepsilon^{-1}$, and $\\log\\delta^{-1}$.*\n\n2.  *Given rational $0<\\tau<1$, the sampling algorithm reports infeasibility exactly when $\\mathcal A$ is empty.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-deletion-sampler",
        "exact_quote": "Given a finite simple undirected unweighted graph $H_0$ whose vertices and edges are explicitly listed, and a rational $0<\\tau<1$, there is a uniform classical randomized algorithm that reports infeasibility exactly when $H_0$ has no perfect matching.         }

      "exact_statement": "Given a finite simple undirected unweighted graph $H_0$ whose vertices and edges are explicitly listed, and a rational $0<\\tau<1$, there is a uniform classical randomized algorithm that reports infeasibility exactly when $H_0$ has no perfect matching. Otherwise it returns a perfect matching on every execution, and its output law is at total variation distance at most $\\tau$ from the uniform perfect-matching law. Its bit running time on every execution is polynomial in the full input encoding length and $\\tau^{-1}$.",
      "explanation": "Edge-by-edge deletion with feasibility tests and approximate counts yields a perfect matching on every execution whose law is close in total variation to uniform, in time polynomial in the input and the inverse tolerance.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **tree-bag graph** ([source](#stmt-def-tree-bags)): A *tree-bag graph* consists of a finite rooted tree of labels and a finite even vertex set.
- **balanced activities** ([source](#sec:holes)): The activities $a$ are *balanced* if $1/4\le r_i(a)\le1$ for every $i$.
- **bottleneck similarity** ([source](#pm-h-7)): For a positive weighted connected graph define $$\label{eq:bottleneck} B_{ij}=\max\left\{1,\ \max_{P:i\leadsto j}\ \min_{e\in P}a_e\right\} \quad (i\ne j).$$
- **holes** ([source](#pm-h-6)): We call the vertices in $U$ *holes*.
- **real edges** ([source](#pm-h-12)): We call these the *real* edges and write $Z'$ and $g'$ for their partition function and hole ratios.
- **probe** ([source](#pm-h-13)): For each logical edge $e$, split off activity $1/D$ from the virtual activity on its central colored edge, and mark that portion as the *probe for $e$*.
- **broken path** ([source](#pm-h-14)): A path is *broken* if at least one of its internal vertices is deleted.
- **admissible arc** ([source](#sec:cells)): An arc is *admissible* if its endpoints $u,v$ satisfy $S_u\cap S_v\ne\varnothing$.
- **good arc** ([source](#sec:cells)): Call $J$ *good* if it is short or if $$S_{v_1}\cap S_{v_{s-1}}\ne\varnothing.$$
- **switch demand** ([source](#pm-h-23)): A *switch demand* specifies $(I_1,I_2)$, a simple discrepancy cycle $C$, and one cell of its fixed quadrangulation.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-D0 | D_0 | = | $10^8(n+1)^4$ | n even, at least two |
| q-two-hole-bound | hole ratio of a two-vertex set | ≤ | $4$ | two holes |
| q-four-hole-bound | hole ratio of a four-vertex set | ≤ | $48$ | four holes |
| q-relocation-bound | hole ratio of a two- or four-hole set | ≤ | $\frac{10^5n}{B_{ij}}$ | distinct holes i, j in U |
| q-bag-inflation | inflation factor I | ≤ | $2$ | balanced logical activities |
| q-path-length-p | path parameter p | = | $2K+2$ | K a nonnegative integer |
| q-enlarged-vertices | enlarged vertex count N | = | $n+4p\binom n2$ | n, p as defined |
| q-bag-scale-D | bag scale D | = | $100N^2D_0$ | N, D_0 as defined |
| q-cell-comparability-factor | activity ratio factor A_0 | = | $1000D$ | any common clamp interval |
| q-preimage-bound | preimage bound T | = | $(10N)^{30}$ | N at least two |
| q-poincare-constant-L | pair energy constant L | = | $(10^4ND)^{100}$ | N, D as defined |
| q-replica-gap | spectral gap of the lazy product chain | ≥ | $3/(8M)$ | M number of updates |
| q-slots-per-tier | slots per tier q | = | $32L$ | L as defined |
| q-update-count | update count M | = | $Tq^2+q$ | T tiers, q slots |
| q-annealing-base | annealing base b | = | $1+\frac1n$ | vertex count n |
| q-balance-floor | truncation floor alpha | = | $10^{-5}$ | all stages |
| q-stage-error-tolerance | relative error of one ratio estimate | ≤ | $\frac\varepsilon{100K}$ | empirical means within sigma |
| q-trial-count | number of independent trials | = | $10(\ell+1)+1$ | confidence parameter delta |
| q-degree-fibre | fibre size of the prescribed-degree gadget | = | $\prod_v(d_v-f_v)!$ | valid demands |
| q-cardinality-fibre | fibre size of the fixed-cardinality gadget | = | $(n-2k)!$ | zero to half of n |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-four-holes** [claim](#stmt-lem-four-holes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-four-holes): Switch alternating paths in an overlay of two matchings; injective comparisons -> four-hole ratio bounded by products of two-hole ratios
- **s-hole-relocation** [claim](#stmt-lem-hole-relocation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hole-relocation): Step-by-step relocation of a hole along a strong path with normalization by row maxima -> hole ratio inversely proportional to bottleneck similarity
- **s-logical-hole-bound** [claim](#stmt-thm-logical-hole-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hole-relocation-2): Interpolate added activities, differentiate the partition function, then insert a maximal pairing -> bound on every even hole set
- **s-subdivision** [claim](#stmt-lem-subdivision) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-subdivision): Two tilings per intact path with ratio equal to the logical activity -> matching bijection and comparable effective strengths
- **s-marked-laws** [claim](#stmt-prop-marked-laws) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-marked-laws): Event identities with constant weight multiplier -> all-real probability and probe probability identities
- **s-deletion-accounting** [claim](#stmt-lem-deletion-accounting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-deletion-accounting): Parity analysis of holes on broken paths with prefix ratios -> deletion identity with factor and logical hole set
- **s-ultrametric-pairing** [claim](#stmt-lem-ultrametric-pairing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-ultrametric-pairing): Threshold integral representation and laminar greedy pairing -> threshold formula for pairing capacity
- **s-lifted-hole-bound** [claim](#stmt-lem-lifted-hole-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-lifted-hole-bound): Charge floor counts per threshold and integrate -> hole bound for enlarged vertices
- **s-bag-inflation** [claim](#stmt-prop-bag-inflation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-lifted-hole-bound-2): Expand over partial matchings of virtual edges and sum a geometric series -> inflation factor at most two
- **s-quadrangulation** [claim](#stmt-lem-quadrangulation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-quadrangulation): Recursive splitting of odd arcs using label-tree neighbor arguments -> label-adapted quadrangulation
- **s-cell-comparability** [claim](#stmt-lem-cell-comparability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cell-comparability): Tree distance bound then height ratio and clamping monotonicity -> bounded activity ratios within a cell
- **s-elementary-energy** [claim](#stmt-lem-elementary-pair-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-elementary-pair-energy): Proposal and acceptance lower bounds for each move type -> energy bounds for switches, color changes, cycle swaps
- **s-cell-identity** [claim](#stmt-lem-cell-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cell-identity): Canonical gains and cancellation across complementary arcs -> exact signed identity for a whole cycle
- **s-encoding** [claim](#stmt-lem-encoding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-encoding): Repair operation and tagged inverse counting preimages -> bounded-preimage encodings with bounded weight distortion
- **s-swap-sum** [claim](#stmt-lem-swap-sum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-swap-sum): Identify each identity with a whole discrepancy cycle and compare with cycle-swap energy -> bound on the swap sum
- **s-pair-energy** [claim](#stmt-thm-pair-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-swap-sum-2): Cycle-by-cycle decomposition, Cauchy-Schwarz and combination of switch, error and swap loads -> variance bounded by the pair energy
- **s-base-refresh** [claim](#stmt-lem-base-refresh) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-base-refresh): Tree dynamic program with convolution polynomials and proportional sampling -> exact count and exact sample at unit activities
- **s-replica-gap** [claim](#stmt-lem-replica-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-replica-gap): Martingale increments, Efron-Stein orthogonality of residuals, absorption by replication -> spectral gap for the product chain
- **s-finite-random-choice** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-random-choice) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-random-choice): Grid-point counting in cumulative intervals -> total variation error of a finite-bit choice
- **s-bag-sampler** [claim](#stmt-thm-bag-sampler) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-bag-sampler): Spectral mixing bound from the gap and minimum mass, then finite-bit coupling -> sampler within the target total variation distance
- **s-one-pass-scaling** [claim](#stmt-lem-one-pass-scaling) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-one-pass-scaling): Feasibility invariant and persistence of a tight constraint -> scale vector with tight rows
- **s-successful-stage** [claim](#stmt-lem-successful-stage) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-successful-stage): Perturbation of observable ratios under accurate means, then scaling bounds -> balance preserved and ratio error bounded
- **s-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-38): Annealing product of ratio estimates, Hoeffding concentration, median amplification -> the approximation scheme with bounded bit time
- **s-deletion-sampler** [claim](#stmt-lem-deletion-sampler) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-deletion-sampler): Self-reduction by edge deletion with approximate counts and dyadic branch probabilities -> near-uniform perfect matching sampler
- **s-prescribed-degree** [claim](#stmt-cor-prescribed-degree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-deletion-sampler-2): Constant-fibre gadget reductions to perfect matchings, then division by the fibre size -> counting and sampling for prescribed-degree and fixed-size families

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-scaling-range**: After the single pass over the vertices in their fixed order, every final scaling coordinate lies in the closed interval from half of the lower activity floor up to two, and every coordinate only decreases from its initial value.
- **inv-scaling-dominance**: The final vector satisfies the product constraint on every off-diagonal pair: the product of the two coordinates is at least the corresponding array entry.
- **inv-scaling-row-tightness**: In every row of the final vector at least one product constraint holds with equality, so no coordinate can be lowered without violating a constraint.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-array-out-of-range**: Run the one-pass update on an array whose off-diagonal entries exceed three or fall below the floor and still rely on the stated coordinate range. -> The first update can produce a coordinate above two or below half the floor, so the range guarantee is lost.
- **anti-balance-unconditional**: Assume the scaled activities are balanced after a stage regardless of how accurate the empirical estimates were. -> The balance and relative-error conclusion is only proved for stages whose empirical means are close to their exact means; otherwise nothing is guaranteed.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The one-pass scaling procedure is an explicit finite sequence of exact rational operations over a fixed vertex order, so its output and the three stated properties can be computed and decided exactly on concrete arrays.

```python
# INPUTS: stdin tokens: n, alpha, then n*n entries of a symmetric array G as rationals (diagonal ignored).
# PRECONDITIONS: n >= 2; G symmetric; alpha <= G[i][j] <= 3 for i != j; alpha > 0.
# OUTPUTS: one line with n space-separated rationals v[0..n-1] produced by the one-pass update in the fixed vertex order.
# TIME: O(n^2) rational operations.
# SPACE: O(n^2) rationals for the stored array.
# ARITHMETIC: exact rationals via the standard fractions module; no rounding anywhere.
# FAILURE: malformed input or violated preconditions raise an exception and give a nonzero exit status.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction


def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    n = int(data[0])
    G = [[Fraction(data[2 + i * n + j]) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
    v = [Fraction(2)] * n
    for i in range(n):
        v[i] = max(G[i][j] / v[j] for j in range(n) if j != i)
    print(" ".join(str(x) for x in v))


main()
```

| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The scheme is a proof device with astronomically large polynomial degrees, so a direct library implementation would be unusable in practice; the source says its constants favour transparent estimates over practical efficiency. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns game state, physics or interactive simulation; objects counted are labeled edge subsets of explicit graphs, not simulated entities. |

| algorithms | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The paper constructs a finite randomized procedure for approximate counting and, via self-reduction, near-uniform generation of perfect matchings, with explicit stages, parameters and an every-execution bit bound. |
| data_structures | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The unit-activity base tier is handled by a dynamic program over a rooted tree of labels, with per-bag count tables indexed by how many interface vertices are assigned to the bag. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The source accounts only for schoolbook bit-cost arithmetic and gives no hardware mapping or accelerator design. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No cryptographic primitive, hardness assumption or security definition appears; the only hardness remark is a classical counting-complexity background fact. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no learning problem, loss or training procedure; the sampler targets a weighted matching law on an explicit graph. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The task is unweighted counting and sampling, not optimising a weighted matching objective; activities are annealing devices, not costs. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The algorithm is a single-machine classical randomized procedure; no communication, fault or consensus model is considered. |
| other (approximate counting and near-uniform sampling of degree-constrained subgraphs) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Constant-fibre gadget reductions turn prescribed-degree subgraphs and fixed-size matchings into perfect-matching instances, giving counting and total-variation sampling guarantees on arbitrary explicit simple hosts. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-balance-required-for-hole-bound** (hypothesis): The logical hole bound and everything built on it (bounded inflation, lifted hole bound, stage-wise accuracy) are proved only for balanced activities, meaning every row maximum of two-hole ratios lies in a fixed interval. Without balance the bound on every even hole set is not available. Blocked use: ['Applying the hole bound or the factor-two inflation bound to arbitrary positive activities that have not been rebalanced', 'Reusing the all-real probability lower bound on an unbalanced weighted graph']
- **lim-parameter-ranges-eps-delta** (hypothesis): The approximation guarantee is stated only for rational error and confidence parameters in the open ranges given in the theorem; the running-time bound is polynomial in the inverse error and the logarithm of the inverse failure probability, not for arbitrary parameter values. Blocked use: ['Invoking the scheme with a relative error of at least one or a failure probability of at least one half', 'Treating the running time as polynomial in the inverse failure probability itself rather than its logarithm']
- **lim-simple-unweighted-input-model** (formal_scope): The input model is a finite simple undirected unweighted graph given explicitly. The paper does not assert a counting algorithm for compressed edge multiplicities or for loops covering singletons. Blocked use: ['Counting perfect matchings of multigraphs given with compressed multiplicities', 'Treating weighted-matching or loop-containing instances as covered by the main theorem']
- **lim-tv-sampling-only** (approximation): The samplers for prescribed-degree subgraphs and fixed-size matchings are guaranteed only within a chosen total variation distance, with cost polynomial in the inverse of that distance. No exact or pointwise almost-uniform guarantee is claimed. Blocked use: ['Using the sampler where exact uniformity or a pointwise multiplicative guarantee is required', 'Assuming running time is polynomial in the logarithm of the inverse tolerance']
- **lim-stage-accuracy-conditional** (hypothesis): Preservation of balance and the relative error of a ratio estimate are proved only for stages whose empirical means are all close to their exact means; on other histories the algorithm still runs in the same time but no accuracy is asserted for that stage. Blocked use: ['Assuming scaled activities are balanced after a stage regardless of estimate accuracy', 'Reading the stage error bound as a guarantee on every random history']
- **lim-scaling-array-range** (edge_case): The one-pass scaling lemma requires a symmetric array whose off-diagonal entries lie between the floor and three. Outside this range the stated coordinate interval and tightness conclusions are not guaranteed. Blocked use: ['Running the update on an unclipped array and relying on the coordinate range', 'Applying it to a non-symmetric array']
- **lim-pairing-capacity-proof-only** (nonconstructive): The pairing capacity is used only inside proofs; the algorithm never enumerates pairings or evaluates the capacity of a general hole set, so it is not a computable certificate of the bound. Blocked use: ['Using the pairing capacity as a runtime check or certificate for a hole set', 'Assuming a polynomial procedure for computing the maximum over all pairings is supplied']
- **lim-impractical-constants** (approximation): The polynomial degrees and constants are chosen for transparent estimates, not practical efficiency, so the result establishes polynomial time in principle rather than a usable implementation. Blocked use: ['Deploying the scheme as a practical matching counter', 'Benchmarking running time claims against practical solvers']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 113 (`lean/docs/113.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A Fully Polynomial Randomized Approximation Scheme for Perfect Matchings in General Graphs](../../preprints/A-Fully-Polynomial-Randomized-Approximation-Scheme-for-Perfect-Matchings-in-General-Graphs-September-23-2026/main.pdf)
> - [Entropy and Face Dimension of the Perfect-Matching Polytope](../../preprints/Entropy-and-Face-Dimension-of-the-Perfect-Matching-Polytope-September-23-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization gives a fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme for counting perfect matchings in every finite simple undirected graph. For rational $0<\varepsilon<1$ and $0<\delta<1/2$, it returns a nonnegative rational estimate with relative error at most $\varepsilon$ with probability at least $1-\delta$. If the graph has no perfect matching, every execution returns zero.
>
> The algorithm is a fixed finite-alphabet randomized machine. Its worst-case running time is polynomial in the binary input length, $\varepsilon^{-1}$, and $\log(\delta^{-1})$, including on unsuccessful random tapes.
>
> For feasible edge marginals $x$ of perfect matchings in a loopless multigraph on $2m$ vertices, let $F(x)=-\sum_e x_e\log x_e$, $B(x)=-\sum_e(1-x_e)\log(1-x_e)$, and let $H(x)$ be the maximum entropy of a matching law with those marginals. The formalization proves $F(x)-(2-2/m)B(x)\le H(x)\le F(x)$ for $m\ge1$, including boundary points of the polytope. It retains the earlier coefficient-eight entropy bound and the deterministic counting approximations with factors $512^n$ for loopless graphs and $2^{18n}$ with singleton loops.
>
> A further statement identifies the exact face obtained by expanding a degree-three vertex into a triangle and shows that minimal faces are carried to minimal faces by the expansion. The paper's sharp global face-dimension bound is not part of that selected statement.

Comparator source `MatchingFPRAS.lean` (4429 bytes, sha256 `14624569e906d3702564c39dab9c63bc77ef1d0b621bd1b51a1239fcafbca206`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace MatchingFPRAS

/-- Each unordered edge is represented once, by increasing endpoints. -/
structure GraphInput where
  n : ℕ
  edges : Finset (Fin n × Fin n)
  increasing : ∀ e ∈ edges, e.1 < e.2

/-- Exactly one selected edge is incident to every vertex. -/
def Perfect (G : GraphInput) (M : Finset (Fin G.n × Fin G.n)) : Prop :=
  M ⊆ G.edges ∧ ∀ v : Fin G.n, ∃! e, e ∈ M ∧ (e.1 = v ∨ e.2 = v)

noncomputable def perfectMatchings (G : GraphInput) : Finset (Finset (Fin G.n × Fin G.n)) :=
  by
    classical
    exact G.edges.powerset.filter (Perfect G)

/-- The exact unweighted count, including the empty graph. -/
noncomputable def Z (G : GraphInput) : ℕ := (perfectMatchings G).card

abbrev Symbol := Fin 8

/-- Binary numeral followed by a delimiter. Tape blank is 0; bits use 1 and 2. -/
def encodeNat (n : ℕ) : List Symbol :=
  (Nat.bits n).map (fun b => if b then 2 else 1) ++ [3]

def encodeInt (z : ℤ) : List Symbol :=
  (if z < 0 then [5] else [4]) ++ encodeNat z.natAbs

/-- A rational encoded by its reduced numerator and positive denominator. -/
def encodeRat (q : ℚ) : List Symbol := encodeInt q.num ++ encodeNat q.den

/-- Explicit sparse graph encoding, then the two rational parameters. -/
def encodeInput (G : GraphInput) (ε δ : ℚ) : List Symbol :=
  encodeNat G.n ++ encodeNat G.edges.card ++
    (((G.edges.map toLex.toEmbedding).sort (· ≤ ·)).flatMap fun e =>
      encodeNat (ofLex e).1.val ++ encodeNat (ofLex e).2.val) ++
    encodeRat ε ++ encodeRat δ

/-- A uniform randomized Post-Turing machine: a single finite transition table. -/
structure RandomMachine where
  states : ℕ
  transition : Fin (states + 1) → Symbol → Bool →
    Option (Fin (states + 1) × Turing.TM0.Stmt Symbol)

structure Configuration (A : RandomMachine) where
  state : Option (Fin (A.states + 1))
  tape : Turing.Tape Symbol

/-- A halted configuration is absorbing. Each nonhalting tick is exactly one
write or one move, or the detection of halt. -/
def tick (A : RandomMachine) (c : Configuration A) (bit : Bool) : Configuration A :=
  match c.state with
  | none => c
  | some s => match A.transition s c.tape.head bit with
    | none => ⟨none, c.tape⟩
    | some (s', action) =>
      ⟨some s', match action with
        | .move d => c.tape.move d
        | .write a => c.tape.write a⟩

def initial (A : RandomMachine) (input : List Symbol) : Configuration A :=
  ⟨some 0, Turing.Tape.mk₁ input⟩

/-- Bounded execution on a finite prefix of the random tape. -/
def run (A : RandomMachine) (input : List Symbol) {t : ℕ}
    (bits : Fin t → Bool) : Configuration A :=
  (List.ofFn bits).foldl (tick A) (initial A input)

def Outputs (A : RandomMachine) (input : List Symbol) {t : ℕ}
    (bits : Fin t → Bool) (q : ℚ) : Prop :=
  (run A input bits).state = none ∧
    (run A input bits).tape.right₀ = Turing.ListBlank.mk (encodeRat q)

/-- A fixed-degree polynomial in input bits, ε⁻¹, and log δ⁻¹.
Natural ceilings and binary ceiling-log change this bound only by constants. -/
def timeBound (C d : ℕ) (G : GraphInput) (ε δ : ℚ) : ℕ :=
  C * ((encodeInput G ε δ).length + ⌈ε⁻¹⌉₊ + Nat.clog 2 ⌈δ⁻¹⌉₊ + 1) ^ d

noncomputable def goodTapes (A : RandomMachine) (G : GraphInput) (ε δ : ℚ)
    (t : ℕ) : Finset (Fin t → Bool) := by
  classical
  exact Finset.univ.filter fun bits => ∃ q : ℚ,
    Outputs A (encodeInput G ε δ) bits q ∧
    (1 - ε) * (Z G : ℚ) ≤ q ∧ q ≤ (1 + ε) * (Z G : ℚ)

/-- A uniform approximation scheme using independent fair bits. All tapes halt
within the fixed polynomial bound and return a nonnegative rational.
A zero count always produces encoded zero. -/
def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ (A : RandomMachine) (C d : ℕ), 0 < C ∧
    ∀ (G : GraphInput) (ε δ : ℚ), 0 < ε → ε < 1 → 0 < δ → δ < 1 / 2 →
      let t := timeBound C d G ε δ
      (∀ bits : Fin t → Bool, ∃ q : ℚ, 0 ≤ q ∧ Outputs A (encodeInput G ε δ) bits q) ∧
      (Z G = 0 → ∀ bits : Fin t → Bool, Outputs A (encodeInput G ε δ) bits 0) ∧
      ((goodTapes A G ε δ t).card : ℚ) / (2 : ℚ) ^ t ≥ 1 - δ


/-- A fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme for perfect matchings
in every finite simple undirected graph. -/
theorem thm_main : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end MatchingFPRAS

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme (FPRAS) for counting perfect matchings in arbitrary finite simple undirected graphs, resolving the general-graph perfect-matching approximation problem. The algorithm returns zero with certainty when no perfect matching exists. Otherwise, it achieves relative error <i>ε</i> with failure probability at most <i>δ</i> in worst-case bit time polynomial in the input length, $`\varepsilon ^{-1}`$, and $`\log\delta^{-1}`$.

| [stmt-lem-finite-random-choice](#stmt-lem-finite-random-choice) | lemma | `lem:finite-random-choice` | 24 | Let $p_1,\ldots,p_r$ be nonnegative rational numbers summing to one. |
| [stmt-cor-prescribed-degree](#stmt-cor-prescribed-degree) | corollary | `cor:prescribed-degree` | 25 | For either family $\mathcal A=\mathcal F(G,f)$ or $\mathcal A=\mathcal M_k(G)$, there are uniform classical randomized algorithms with the following guarantees.* |
| [stmt-lem-deletion-sampler](#stmt-lem-deletion-sampler) | lemma | `lem:deletion-sampler` | 26 | Given a finite simple undirected unweighted graph $H_0$ whose vertices and edges are explicitly listed, and a rational $0<\tau<1$, there is a uniform classical randomized algorithm that reports infeas |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $G=(V,E)$ be a finite simple undirected graph. A perfect matching is a set of edges incident to each vertex exactly once. Write $$Z(G)=\#\{M\subseteq E:M\text{ is a perfect matching of }G\}.$$ A fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme approximates $Z(G)$ within a prescribed relative error, with a prescribed success probability, in time polynomial in the input length and the reciprocal error. The confidence parameter enters through its logarithm. We prove the following theorem, including a bound on the time of every execution.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a uniform classical randomized algorithm which, given $G$ and rational parameters $0<\varepsilon<1$ and $0<\delta<1/2$, returns a nonnegative rational number $\widehat Z$ such that $$\mathbb P\bigl[(1-\varepsilon)Z(G)\le \widehat Z\le(1+\varepsilon)Z(G)\bigr]
 \ge 1-\delta.$$ If $Z(G)=0$, the output is zero with certainty. The worst-case bit running time is polynomial in the input encoding length, $\varepsilon^{-1}$, and $\log\delta^{-1}$.*

</div>

As consequences, <a href="#sec:prescribed-degree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prescribed-degree">10</a> gives counting schemes for edge subsets with prescribed vertex degrees and for matchings of a specified size. On feasible inputs, its samplers always return a feasible object and approximate the corresponding uniform law in total variation.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and significance

Perfect matchings illustrate the distinction between finding a combinatorial structure and counting its realizations. Edmonds gave a polynomial-time algorithm for finding a maximum matching in a general graph <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Edmonds1965](#ref-Edmonds1965)]. Valiant proved that exact counting is \#P-complete even for bipartite graphs, where it is the permanent of a zero–one matrix <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Valiant1979](#ref-Valiant1979)]. Pfaffian methods give polynomial-time exact counting for planar graphs <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Kasteleyn1963](#ref-Kasteleyn1963)].

For approximation, three problems must be distinguished: counting all matchings, counting bipartite perfect matchings, and counting perfect matchings in general graphs. Jerrum and Sinclair gave an FPRAS for the total number of matchings in an arbitrary graph, summed over all cardinalities <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[JerrumSinclair1989](#ref-JerrumSinclair1989), Corollary 4.5]. Their perfect-matching algorithm applied when the ratio of near-perfect matchings, which leave exactly two vertices unmatched, to perfect matchings was bounded by a fixed polynomial <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[JerrumSinclair1989](#ref-JerrumSinclair1989), Theorem 5.3]. Without such a bound, perfect matchings can carry too little stationary mass for this sampling approach. Approximating the total number of matchings therefore does not isolate its perfect-matching contribution with relative accuracy.

Jerrum, Sinclair, and Vigoda removed the ratio restriction in the bipartite setting and obtained an FPRAS for the permanent of every nonnegative matrix <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[JerrumSinclairVigoda2004](#ref-JerrumSinclairVigoda2004), Theorem 1.1]. Their method assigns weights to near-perfect states according to their two unmatched vertices and learns suitable weights while gradually changing edge activities. The general-graph question was explicitly raised by Jerrum and Sinclair <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[JerrumSinclair1989](#ref-JerrumSinclair1989), Section 7(i)] and remained a benchmark for approximate counting. Cai and Liu relate it to approximation for the eight-vertex model <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[CaiLiu2020](#ref-CaiLiu2020)]; Fei, Goldberg, and Lu distinguish the spin-system parameter line equivalent to general perfect-matching approximation from the region covered by their FPRAS <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[FeiGoldbergLu2025](#ref-FeiGoldbergLu2025), Proposition 1.2 and Theorem 1.5]. [thm:main](#thm:main) answers the general-graph perfect-matching approximation problem affirmatively.

A direct extension of the bipartite chain faces a precise obstruction. Štefankovič, Vigoda, and Wilmes constructed graphs on which every JSV-type chain has either exponentially small stationary probability of perfect matchings or exponentially slow mixing, even with arbitrary weights depending on the hole pattern <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[StefankovicVigodaWilmes2018](#ref-StefankovicVigodaWilmes2018), Definition 2.1 and Theorem 2.2 in the full version]. Here JSV type means the perfect-and-near-perfect state space with the specified Broder proposals and a Metropolis acceptance rule. The sampler below uses a different state space: a product of perfect-matching spaces on an enlarged colored graph, with exchanges of alternating cycles between coordinates. Its energy comparison is proved for these states and moves.

Subsequent permanent algorithms sharpen the bipartite result. Bezáková, Štefankovič, Vazirani, and Vigoda accelerated the cooling schedule and improved the analysis of the matching chain <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BezakovaStefankovicVaziraniVigoda2008](#ref-BezakovaStefankovicVaziraniVigoda2008)]. Chen, Vigoda, and Yang obtain a further improvement through restricted Poincaré inequalities and coupled flows <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[ChenVigodaYang2026](#ref-ChenVigodaYang2026)]; Chen, Guo, Vigoda, and Yang develop an energy-weighted multicommodity-flow comparison and improve the running time again <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ChenGuoVigodaYang2026](#ref-ChenGuoVigodaYang2026)]. These results concern bipartite perfect matchings.

For nonbipartite graphs, Yi gives deterministic relative approximation for hafnians, the weighted sums over perfect matchings, when the $n$-vertex support has minimum degree at least $(1/2+\gamma)n$ and nonzero weights lie in $[\theta,1]$ <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Yi2026](#ref-Yi2026), Theorem 1.1]. Here $0<\gamma<1/2$ and $0<\theta\le1$ are fixed constants, and the polynomial running-time exponent may depend on them. [thm:main](#thm:main) has no density promise on its simple unweighted input.

The proof below also builds on the methodological framework of weighted matching chains. Alternating overlays and weight-preserving switches underlie the encodings of Jerrum and Sinclair and of Jerrum, Sinclair, and Vigoda <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[JerrumSinclair1989](#ref-JerrumSinclair1989), [JerrumSinclairVigoda2004](#ref-JerrumSinclairVigoda2004)]. Their sampling-to-counting strategy learns local partition ratios while gradually changing activities. Here these ratios are exposed by marked edges in an enlarged graph, and vertex scaling maintains a row-wise balance condition. The capacity bound, subdivision and marked-edge identities needed for this construction are proved explicitly.

Energy comparison through weighted demands has classical antecedents in multicommodity-flow bounds for mixing <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Sinclair1992](#ref-Sinclair1992)]. Our comparison differs from routing every demand along a path of chain transitions: long-arc conversions enter a signed identity and need not be legal single updates. The resulting estimate initially controls only additive functions of two matching coordinates. The separate product-space argument is essential to obtain a spectral gap for arbitrary functions. It uses the classical orthogonal decomposition into coordinate interactions <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[EfronStein1981](#ref-EfronStein1981), Section 2]; the ordered assignment of residual terms and the replication estimate are proved below.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof architecture

The counting algorithm starts with the complete graph, whose perfect matchings are easy to count, and gradually suppresses the nonedges of $G$ by decreasing their positive activities. It estimates the ratios of successive partition functions and multiplies them. To keep the observations statistically useful, it updates vertex scales from estimates of the two-hole partition ratios: partition functions after deleting two vertices, divided by the current partition function. Accurate estimates keep each row maximum between two absolute constants. A vertex scale multiplies every complete matching weight by the same factor, preserving the probability law used to estimate the current counting ratio.

The purpose of balance is to control the extra mass introduced by an auxiliary graph. We replace each logical edge by a weighted path, with a bijection between logical perfect matchings and matchings using only these real path edges. We then add weak clique edges in overlapping bags indexed by a tree. Under balance, the added edges increase the partition function by at most a factor of two, so a sample uses only real edges with probability at least one half. A specially marked auxiliary edge exposes the corresponding two-hole partition ratio. These are exact event identities; they provide both the counting estimate and the data for the next scale update.

To prove the inflation bound, <a href="#thm:logical-hole-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:logical-hole-bound">3</a> assigns a pairing capacity to each even hole set using the strongest bottleneck paths between its vertices. The capacity pays for the weights of possible added edges on those holes. Subdivision transfers this bound to the real graph while making incident effective strengths comparable. The resulting geometry supplies the tree of clique bags. Thus the hole estimates control the probability of the observable events, while the tree-bag structure supplies a sampler for their law.

That sampler uses local switches in one matching and exchanges alternating cycles between two matching coordinates. Its energy comparison initially controls only additive functions of the two coordinates. A quadrangulation adapted to the bag labels gives an exact signed identity for a whole-cycle difference ([lem:quadrangulation,thm:pair-energy] [lem:quadrangulation](#lem:quadrangulation), [thm:pair-energy](#thm:pair-energy)). The identity is not a route through chain transitions: long arc conversions may change many edges. Two-matching encodings instead compare its terms with local switches and cycle exchanges, with polynomially bounded multiplicity.

A ladder of nearby weight distributions then places many independent coordinates at each tier. An ordered orthogonal decomposition ensures that each coordinate-interaction term is charged to at most one pair. Replication reduces the coefficient of their combined contribution enough to absorb it. This yields a spectral gap for arbitrary functions of the product state (<a href="#lem:replica-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-gap">20</a>). The bottom tier has unit activities and admits exact counting and ideal sampling by a tree dynamic program. The sampler requires no balance assumption. It therefore remains defined, with the same time bound, after an inaccurate empirical estimate; balance is used only to prove that accurate estimates propagate through the counting stages.

summarizes the changes of law. Finally, [sec:counting,sec:complexity] [sec:counting](#sec:counting), [sec:complexity](#sec:complexity) account for all arithmetic and finite-bit random choices. Every polynomial has an absolute degree; the constants are chosen for transparent estimates rather than practical efficiency.

<div id="tab:graph-laws">

| Object         | Vertices | Relation of matching laws                                                                              |
|:---------------|:--------:|:-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Input $G$      |   $n$    | The target is its unweighted count $Z(G)$.                                                             |
| Complete graph |   $n$    | Raw activities $w^{(j)}$ gradually suppress the nonedges of $G$.                                       |
| Vertex scaling |   $n$    | Activities $\lambda$ preserve the normalized raw matching law.                                         |
| Real paths     |   $N$    | Subdivision gives a matching bijection with a common weight multiplier.                                |
| Colored bags   |   $N$    | Their activities $W$ recover the real law when every sampled edge is revealed as real.                 |
| Weight tiers   |   $N$    | Common-interval clamps of $W$ connect the unit-activity law to the bag law on one colored state space. |

The successive graphs and probability laws. Here $n$ is the original vertex count and $N$ the enlarged count. Scaling, subdivision, conditioning, and clamping have distinct roles.

</div>

The specialized combinatorial and sampling arguments are proved here. We use Edmonds’s algorithm for the initial matching-existence test and Hoeffding’s bounded-variable inequality for concentration and confidence amplification <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Edmonds1965](#ref-Edmonds1965), [Hoeffding1963](#ref-Hoeffding1963)].

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Preliminaries

Logarithms are natural unless a base is indicated.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Weighted matchings and holes

We allow parallel edges distinguished by colors. A matching specifies the actual colored edges it uses. For positive rational activities $a=(a_e)$, put $$\operatorname{wt}_a(M)=\prod_{e\in M}a_e,\qquad
 Z_a(-U)=\sum_{M\in\mathcal M(H-U)}\operatorname{wt}_a(M),
 \qquad Z_a=Z_a(-\varnothing),$$ where $H$ is the underlying graph and $\mathcal M(H-U)$ is its set of perfect matchings after deleting $U$. The empty graph has one perfect matching, of weight one. Whenever $Z_a>0$, define $$g_a(U)=\frac{Z_a(-U)}{Z_a},\qquad
 \pi_a(M)=\frac{\operatorname{wt}_a(M)}{Z_a}.$$ These weighted colored graphs are intermediate spaces constructed from the simple input graph. Their representation is explicit: vertices, colored edges, and rational activities are stored individually. The main theorem retains the finite simple undirected input model; it does not assert a counting algorithm for compressed edge multiplicities or for singleton-covering loops.

We call the vertices in $U$ *holes*. For distinct vertices we abbreviate $g_a(\{i_1,\ldots,i_k\})$ to $g_a(i_1\cdots i_k)$. Collapsing parallel activities into their sum preserves every partition function. Conditional on a collapsed edge, its original type can be recovered with probabilities proportional to the summands.

The union of two matchings, with common edges retained twice, has maximum degree two. Its nontrivial components are alternating paths and even alternating cycles. Swapping the layers along a component preserves the product of their weights. In the union of two perfect matchings, deleting common identical edges leaves disjoint alternating cycles; different colors on the same pair of vertices form a cycle of length two. We fix a vertex order and a color order once and for all. They resolve every combinatorial tie in the proof.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Bottleneck similarities

<a id="eq:bottleneck"></a>
<a id="eq:pairing-capacity"></a>
For a positive weighted connected graph define $$\label{eq:bottleneck}
 B_{ij}=\max\left\{1,\ \max_{P:i\leadsto j}\ \min_{e\in P}a_e\right\}
 \quad (i\ne j).$$ Simple paths suffice. This symmetric similarity satisfies $B_{ij}\ge\min\{B_{ik},B_{kj}\}$: concatenating two paths and erasing loops cannot lower their minimum activity. For $t>1$, its equivalence classes are the connected components of the graph retaining edges of activity at least $t$. Singletons are included. At $t=1$ we take one class containing every vertex, consistently with the floor at one. For an even set $U$ define its *pairing capacity* $$\label{eq:pairing-capacity}
 \Phi_B(U)=\max_{\mathcal P}\prod_{ij\in\mathcal P}B_{ij},
 \qquad \Phi_B(\varnothing)=1,$$ where $\mathcal P$ runs over pairings of $U$. We use this quantity only in proofs; the counting algorithm never needs to enumerate pairings or compute $\Phi_B(U)$ for a general hole set.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Tree bags

<a id="stmt-def-tree-bags"></a>
<div id="def:tree-bags" class="definition">

<a id="eq:bag-weight-range"></a>
**Definition 2**. A *tree-bag graph* consists of a finite rooted tree of labels and a finite even vertex set. A label $l$ at level $k$ has height $h_l=2^k$; the root has level zero and a child has level one greater than its parent. Each vertex belongs to either one label or two adjacent labels. The bag at $l$ is the set of vertices belonging to $l$. For each unordered pair of distinct vertices and each common label $l$, there is one edge of color $l$. There are no other edges. For an integer parameter $D\ge1$, its reference activities obey $$\label{eq:bag-weight-range}
 \frac{h_l}{D}\le W_{xy,l}\le3h_l.$$ We also consider activities obtained by clamping all $W_e$ to one common interval $[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)$.

</div>

Every vertex pair has at most two edge colors. At any bag, the interfaces with its parent and its different children are disjoint: a vertex in two such interfaces would have at least three memberships. Empty bags and repeated underlying vertex sets are permitted. Clamping is monotone and does not increase the ratio of a larger activity to a smaller one. In particular, reference activities of nearby labels remain comparable after clamping.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Dirichlet energy and approximation

<a id="eq:dirichlet"></a>
<a id="eq:metropolis-capacity"></a>
For a finite reversible Markov kernel $P$ with stationary law $\mu$, its Dirichlet energy is $$\label{eq:dirichlet}
 \mathcal E_{\mu,P}(f)
 =\frac12\sum_{x,y}\mu(x)P(x,y)(f(x)-f(y))^2.$$ A Poincaré inequality $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\mu f\le\gamma^{-1}\mathcal E_{\mu,P}(f)$ means that the spectral gap is at least $\gamma$. A symmetric proposal $Q$ has Metropolis acceptance probability <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[MetropolisEtAl1953](#ref-MetropolisEtAl1953), [Hastings1970](#ref-Hastings1970)] $\min\{1,\mu(y)/\mu(x)\}$ for a proposed move from $x$ to $y$. Its off-diagonal stationary capacity is $$\label{eq:metropolis-capacity}
 \mu(x)P(x,y)=Q(x,y)\min\{\mu(x),\mu(y)\}.$$ We regard inapplicable proposals as holding moves. All state spaces used below contain at least one perfect matching; thus their positive activities define probability laws with full support on that space.

Total variation is $\|\mu-\rho\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{TV}}}=\frac12\sum_x|\mu(x)-\rho(x)|$. A deterministic map, or a common randomized postprocessing kernel, does not increase total variation, by the triangle inequality. Two finite laws at total variation distance $\eta$ admit a coupling that disagrees with probability $\eta$: first match the common masses $\min(\mu(x),\rho(x))$, then couple the remainders. Applying this construction successively bounds the discrepancy probability of an adaptive simulation by the sum of its conditional one-step errors.

<a id="sec:holes"></a>
### Controlling the cost of unmatched vertices

This section proves the bound that will control the additional edges in our sampling graph. It uses only alternating paths and differentiation of a finite partition function.

Let $V$ have even cardinality $n\ge2$, and give every edge of the complete logical graph $K_V$ a positive activity $\lambda_{ij}$. Use the weighted matching notation of <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>, abbreviating $\mathcal M(K_V-U)$ to $\mathcal M(-U)$. For any positive activity array $a$ on this graph, define the row maxima $$r_i(a)=\max_{j\ne i}g_a(ij).$$ The activities $a$ are *balanced* if $1/4\le r_i(a)\le1$ for every $i$. Let $B$ be the bottleneck similarity of $\lambda$ from <a href="#eq:bottleneck" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bottleneck">[eq:bottleneck]</a>, and let $\Phi_B$ be its pairing capacity from <a href="#eq:pairing-capacity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pairing-capacity">[eq:pairing-capacity]</a>.

The estimate we need controls every even hole set, although balance only bounds the two-hole ratios:

<a id="stmt-thm-logical-hole-bound"></a>
<div id="thm:logical-hole-bound" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Logical hole bound). *Let $\lambda$ be balanced, let $B$ be its bottleneck similarity, and set $$D_0=10^8(n+1)^4.$$ For every even set $U\subseteq V$, <a id="eq:logical-hole-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:logical-hole-bound}
 g_\lambda(U)\Phi_B(U)\le 2D_0^{|U|/2}.$$*

</div>

To prove this, we will add a small activity proportional to $B_{ij}$ on every pair. Bounds for two and four holes keep the resulting partition inflation below two. Any pairing of an arbitrary hole set can then be inserted using the added edges, giving the stated bound.

We use two elementary alternating-path comparisons. Such weight-preserving overlays also underlie the weighted matching encodings in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[JerrumSinclair1989](#ref-JerrumSinclair1989), [JerrumSinclairVigoda2004](#ref-JerrumSinclairVigoda2004)]; the comparisons and their constants needed here are proved below. In an overlay of two matchings, edges retain their layer. Common edges can be kept as two parallel copies. A path starting at a vertex unmatched in exactly one layer is then uniquely determined, and switching its edges between the layers preserves the product of their weights.

<a id="stmt-lem-four-holes"></a>
<div id="lem:four-holes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:small-hole-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Four-hole inequality). *For any positive activities $\mu$ and four distinct vertices $a,b,c,d$, $$g_\mu(abcd)\le
 g_\mu(ab)g_\mu(cd)+g_\mu(ac)g_\mu(bd)
                         +g_\mu(ad)g_\mu(bc).$$ Consequently, if every row maximum is at most $4$, then $$\label{eq:small-hole-bounds}
 g_\mu(\varnothing)=1,\qquad
 g_\mu(U)\le4\quad(|U|=2),\qquad
 g_\mu(U)\le48\quad(|U|=4).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-four-holes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-four-holes</summary>

*Proof.* Overlay $M\in\mathcal M(-\{a,b,c,d\})$ and $P\in\mathcal M(-\varnothing)$. The alternating path from $a$ ends at one of $b,c,d$, say $b$. Switch that path between the layers. The output layers miss $\{c,d\}$ and $\{a,b\}$, respectively, and their product weight is unchanged. This map is injective for the specified endpoint $b$: in the output overlay, follow the path from $a$ and switch it back. The same argument applies to endpoints $c,d$. Summing the three injective comparisons and dividing by $Z_\mu^2$ proves the inequality. The remaining assertions follow immediately. ◻

</details>

The four-hole comparison controls small hole sets by row maxima. We next need an additional factor when two holes are joined by a strong path. Normalizing by the row maximum at the moving hole will make the successive relocation errors add, without multiplying along the path.

The next lemma is stated with $\mu\ge\lambda$ so that it continues to apply while we add activities to the graph. Its bottleneck similarity always remains the one defined from $\lambda$.

<a id="stmt-lem-hole-relocation"></a>
<div id="lem:hole-relocation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Relocating a hole along a strong path). *Let $B$ be defined by <a href="#eq:bottleneck" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bottleneck">[eq:bottleneck]</a>, and suppose that $\mu_e\ge\lambda_e$ for every edge and $$\frac18\le r_i(\mu)\le4\qquad(i\in V).$$ If $|U|\in\{2,4\}$ and $i,j\in U$ are distinct, then <a id="eq:hole-relocation-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:hole-relocation-bound}
 g_\mu(U)\le\frac{10^5n}{B_{ij}}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hole-relocation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hole-relocation</summary>

*Proof.* Throughout this proof, omit the subscript $\mu$ from $g$ and $r$. Write $B=B_{ij}$. For $B\le8$, the assertion follows from <a href="#eq:small-hole-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-hole-bounds">[eq:small-hole-bounds]</a>. Suppose therefore that $B>8$, and choose a simple path from $i$ to $j$ whose $\lambda$-activities, and hence its $\mu$-activities, are all at least $B$.

<a id="eq:adjacent-hole-fill"></a>
We first give one step of the relocation. Let $a$ be the hole currently on this path, let $S$ be the current hole set, and let $z$ be the next vertex. Thus $a\in S$ and $\mu_{az}\ge B$. If $z\in S$, inserting $az$ in a matching missing $S$ is injective and increases its weight by $\mu_{az}$. Hence $$\label{eq:adjacent-hole-fill}
 \frac{g(S)}{r_a}\le
 \frac{g(S\setminus\{a,z\})}{B r_a}\le\frac{32}{B}.$$ The last inequality uses $|S|\le4$, <a href="#eq:small-hole-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-hole-bounds">[eq:small-hole-bounds]</a>, and $r_a\ge1/8$.

Now suppose $z\notin S$. Choose $p\ne z$ with $g(zp)=r_z$. Inserting $az$ gives $g(az)\le1/\mu_{az}\le1/B<1/8$, so $p\ne a$. Overlay $M\in\mathcal M(-S)$ and $P\in\mathcal M(-\{z,p\})$. The path from $a$ starts with an edge of $P$. Its endpoint belongs to exactly one of the following three cases.

1.  It ends at $z$. Switching the path gives layers missing $S\setminus\{a\}\cup\{z\}$ and $\{a,p\}$. The product weight is unchanged.

2.  It ends at $p$, which requires $p\notin S$. Switching gives layers missing $S\setminus\{a\}\cup\{p\}$ and $\{a,z\}$. Insert $az$ in the second layer, making it perfect. The product weight increases by $\mu_{az}\ge B$.

3.  It ends at $x\in S\setminus\{a,p\}$. Switching gives layers missing $S\setminus\{a,x\}$ and $\{z,p,a,x\}$. Insert $az$ in the second layer; its remaining holes are $\{p,x\}$. Again the product weight increases by at least $B$.

These cases exhaust the possibilities. Indeed, the degree-one vertices in the overlay are exactly the symmetric difference of the two hole sets; if $p\in S$, then $p$ is isolated and cannot be an endpoint of the path.

The edge $az$ is absent from both input layers, since the first misses $a$ and the second misses $z$. Switching cannot create an edge absent from the overlay. Each map is injective when its case and endpoint are specified. For case (i), the inverse switches the output path from $a$. For cases (ii) and (iii), first remove the inserted edge $az$ from the second output layer, then switch the path from $a$ in the resulting overlay. This reconstructs both input layers, including their common edges. Summing over all inputs, using $g(ap)\le r_a$, and dividing by $Z_\mu^2r_z$ yields <a id="eq:relocation-step"></a>$$\label{eq:relocation-step}
\begin{split}
 g(S)\le{}&\frac{r_a}{r_z}
                g(S\setminus\{a\}\cup\{z\})\\
 &+\frac{1}{B r_z}\left[
   \mathbf 1_{\{p\notin S\}}g(S\setminus\{a\}\cup\{p\})
   +\sum_{x\in S\setminus\{a,p\}}
        g(S\setminus\{a,x\})g(px)\right].
\end{split}$$ An inapplicable first term inside the brackets is omitted entirely. The brackets are at most $96$: for $|S|=4$ their first term is at most $48$ and their at most three product terms are each at most $16$; for $|S|=2$ the corresponding bound is $8$. After division by $r_a$, therefore, <a id="eq:normalized-relocation"></a>$$\label{eq:normalized-relocation}
 \frac{g(S)}{r_a}\le
 \frac{g(S\setminus\{a\}\cup\{z\})}{r_z}
       +\frac{6144}{B}.$$

Starting at $a=i$, apply this recurrence along the chosen path, keeping all holes in $U\setminus\{i\}$ fixed. Stop just before reaching the first of these fixed holes; such a hole exists because the path ends at $j$.
